LARGE AUTOMORPHISM GROUPS COMPARED TO THE p-RANK OF
ALGEBRAIC CURVES IN CHARACTERISTIC p
MASSIMO GIULIETTI, G´ABOR KORCHM´AROS, AND MARCO TIMPANELLA
Abstract. Let X be a (projective, geometrically irreducible, non-singular) algebraic curve of genus ≥2 and
positive p-rank γ(X), defined over an algebraically closed field K of positive characteristic p > 0. Contrary
to what occurs for the genera, no function h(γ) exists such that |Aut(X)| ≤h(γ) whenever γ = γ(X). Thus,
to have a bound on |Aut(X)| only depending on γ(X), some restrictions on X and Aut(X) are needed. In
this context, the following theorem is proven. Let Γ be a subgroup of Aut(X). Assume the existence of
a point P ∈X such that if SP is the Sylow p-subgroup of ΓP fixing P, then the quotient curve X/SP is
rational. Then the following p-rank analog of the Riemann-Hurwitz bound
|Γ| < 900

p −1
4
γ(X)4
holds, unless a subgroup of index ≤2 of Γ fixes P. This bound is sharp apart from the constant.
Keywords: algebraic curve, function field, positive characteristic, automorphism group, p-rank.
Subject classifications: 14H37, 14H05.
1. Introduction
The classical theory of birational invariants of algebraic curves was developed over time, originating
from Riemann’s work on complex curves (Riemann surfaces) in the mid-19th century. The most important
birational invariants of a (projective, geometrically irreducible, non-singular, algebraic) curve X defined over
a field K include its genus g(X) and the order of its automorphism group Aut(X) fixing K elementwise.
The earliest major achievement was the Riemann-Hurwitz formula yielding the upper bound 84(g(X) −1)
on the order of the automorphism group of any complex curve other than rational and elliptic curves. Ever
since, comparison of numerical birational invariants of curves has attracted remarkable interest, and the
extensive literature also contains contributions that may be viewed as refinements of the Riemann-Hurwitz
bound for automorphism groups with special structures, such as cyclic groups and groups whose order is a
prime-power; see [12, 37, 77] and the references therein.
The classical results on birational invariants hold true for curves defined over any field of zero characteris-
tic, whereas the picture changes radically whenever the characteristic is assumed to be positive. A first sign
is that the classical Riemann-Hurwitz bound fails. Although the finiteness of the automorphism group of
any curve other than the rational and elliptic remains valid, nevertheless the Riemann-Hurwitz type upper
bound on |Aut(X)| increases to 16g(X)4, with a unique exception of the Hermitian curve of genus 1
2q(q −1)
whose automorphism group is isomorphic to PGU(3, q) and hence it has order (q3 + 1)q3(q2 −1) where q is
any power of the characteristic; see [62, 63].

As a matter of fact, in positive characteristic, automorphism groups of curves present several different
features in the non-tame case which occurs when the curve X contains a point P such that the stabilizer ΓP
of P in Γ = Aut(X) has a non-trivial p-subgroup. A possible cause and explanation of this phenomenon may
be the structural difference in ΓP , since ΓP is cyclic in zero characteristic while it is the semidirect product
of SP with a cyclic group where SP is the unique (normal) Sylow p-subgroup of ΓP whose structure varies
depending on the local properties of the curve. This is apparent in ramification theory and its applications.
In characteristic p > 0, a third relevant numerical birational invariant is the p-rank (equivalently the
Hasse-Witt invariant) γ(X) of a curve X where 0 ≤γ(X) ≤g(X). From previous work it emerged that
curves with large automorphism groups have zero p-rank, and this raised the problem of determining a
function h(g) defined for g ≥2 such that if |G| > h(g) for a subgroup G ≤Aut(X) of a curve X of genus
g = g(X) then γ(X) = 0. Such a function exists, as Henn’s classification [33] shows that there are only four
curves with |Aut(X)| ≥8g(X)3 and each of them has zero p-rank. It seems plausible that the amplitude
of h(g) may be much smaller, namely h(g) ≈cg2. So far, this has been proven in odd characteristic: for
solvable groups G with c = 126, and for non-solvable groups G with c = 900 under the hypothesis that g is
even; see [24]. In both cases, the structure of Aut(X) was also determined. For p = 2, an analogous result
is not available yet, although a complete classification of the structure of Aut(X) was given [22].
In this paper, the focus is on curves with positive p-rank, and the subject is the analog of the Riemann-
Hurwitz bound on |Aut(X)| where the p-rank replaces the genus. Actually, such a bound does not always
exist; see Example 4, and, for instance, the family of Fermat curves in [10]. Nevertheless, some restriction
may ensure that this does not happen, as it follows from our main result.
Theorem 1.1. Let X be a (projective, geometrically irreducible, non-singular) algebraic curve of genus ≥2
and positive p-rank γ(X), defined over an algebraically closed field K of positive characteristic p > 0. Let Γ
be a subgroup of Aut(X). Assume the existence of a point P ∈X such that
(*) if SP is the Sylow p-subgroup of ΓP fixing P, then the quotient curve X/SP is rational.

The proof of Theorem 1.1 is carried out by a careful analysis of the possible actions of Aut(X) on the non-
tame orbit ∆containing the point P in (*). Doing so, the Riemann-Hurwitz and the Deuring–Shafarevich
formulas and some results from Function field theory, such as Castelnuovo’s bound, are combined with deeper
Group theory relying on a number of results which were the milestones in CSG (the classification of finite
simple groups), and on more recent results on primitive permutation groups depending on CSG.
As for the Riemann-Hurwitz bound, possible refinements of the bound (1) are worth investigating. In
this direction, our main result is stated in the following theorem where ∆0 stands for the set consisting of all
points Q of X, other than P, which are fixed by some non-trivial element of SP . Note that ∆0 is non-empty.
In fact, since γ(X) ≥1, (*) implies that the cover X|(X/SP ) is also ramified at some point Q ̸= P of X,
and therefore ∆0 ̸= ∅by [39, Lemma 11.129].
Theorem 1.2. Let X be a (projective, geometrically irreducible, non-singular) algebraic curve of genus ≥2
and positive p-rank γ(X), defined over an algebraically closed field K of positive characteristic p > 0. Let Γ
be a subgroup of Aut(X). Assume the existence of a point P ∈X such that both (*) and (***) hold where
(***) the orbit ∆of P under the action of Γ coincides with ∆0 ∪{P}, i.e. ∆consists of all points of X
fixed by some non-trivial element of SP .

The paper is organized as follows. Sections 2, 3 fix notation and quote previous results from Algebraic
geometry, Function field theory and Group theory. Sections 4 and 5 establish preliminary results used in
the sequel. Section 6 provides the proofs of Theorems 1.2 and 1.3. It also contains a proof of Theorem 1.1
under the hypothesis (***); to deal with the general case, however, much more is needed and the proof is
carried out in Section 7. Finally, Section 8 gives some examples.
In more details, Section 2 gives an overview of the known Riemann-Hurwitz type bounds. Section 3 recalls
several previous results from Group theory which play a role in the paper, and it also contains the proofs
of some apparently unquoted results from Group theory and Number theory. We begin to get to the heart
of the paper in Section 4: Lemma 4.2 is a new refinement of the Riemann-Hurwitz bound in the spirit of
[24, Lemma 4.1] while Propositions 4.4 and 4.6 classify subgroups of Aut(X) which are doubly transitive
permutation groups on a non-tame orbit.
From Section 5 on, both hypotheses (*) and (**) are assumed where
(**) the p-rank γ(X) of X is positive.

In Section 6, hypothesis (***) is assumed.
First the proof of Theorem 1.2 is given. This theorem makes it possible to suppose in the proofs of
Theorems 1.1 and 1.3 that SP acts on ∆0 as a transitive permutation group. Then Γ is doubly transitive on
∆and the properties of Γ, collected in Proposition 6.4, are stringent enough. In fact, with the use of these
properties, and some technical tools, one gets to Theorem 1.1 through Propositions 6.8, 6.9 and Theorem
6.11. Finally, Example 4 shows that hypothesis |∆| > 2 cannot be dropped in Theorem 1.1.
In Section 7, the case where hypothesis (***) fails is worked out. Unfortunately, bound (4) combined with
previous results are not sufficient to get directly to a proof for Theorem 1.1 except in a few special cases
described in Proposition 7.5. To overcome the impasse, a careful analysis of the possible actions of Γ on ∆
is needed which is carried out in several steps .
The first one consists of proving Theorem 1.1 when either Γ has a unique short orbit, or it has no tame-
orbit, or SP and ΓP have at least two short orbits other than {P}, or the action of Γ on ∆is not faithful;
see Propositions 7.4, 7.5 and Lemmas 7.6, 7.7. For the rest of Section 7, all these cases are dismissed, and
SP is assumed to be a Sylow p-subgroup of Γ by Lemma 5.4.
The second step, motivated by the fact that |Γ| = |ΓP ||∆|, aims at obtaining a linear upper bound on the
size of ∆in terms of g(X) up to a factor depending only on the order of HP where ΓP = SP ⋊HP and HP
is a cyclic group of prime to p order. Such a bound, established in Proposition 7.8, states, apart from two
sporadic cases, that

The proof of Proposition 7.8 is a bit long but elementary and also uses some ideas from previous papers
[53, 51]; see Proposition 7.8. The usefulness of (5) becomes apparent in the successive steps. In particular,
(5) together with (4) and one of the properties from Proposition 6.4, namely γ(X) = |SP |−(|∆|−1), provide
a proof of Theorem 1.1 when |HP | is small, that is, |HP | ≤8, apart from two sporadic cases. Bound (5) is
also a key ingredient in the proof of Proposition 7.10 which shows some more features of the action of Γ. In
particular, if Γ is imprimitive on ∆, which is the case where Permutation group theory may not work well,
Proposition 7.10 shows a useful combinatorial property of the action of Γ on ∆, namely either ∆0 ∪{P} is
a block, or it is contained in any block through P.
After some preliminary results on the structure of SP ; see Propositions 7.11, 7.12, 7.13 and 7.14, the third
step is to settle the case where Γ is a primitive permutation group on ∆. Theorem 7.15 and Proposition
7.17 show that Γ may be assumed to have a simple non-abelian normal subgroup N containing SP that is
still primitive on ∆. As N has trivial centralizer in Γ, it turns out that the group Γ may be supposed to
be almost simple with socle N; see Theorem 7.18. For p = 2, this together with the Hering-Shult theorem,
stated as Result 3.15, yields that N is double transitive on ∆whence ∆= ∆0 ∪{P} follows; in particular
(***) does not fail. For p > 2, we rely on the classification [50] of the pairs (G, H) where G is a simple
primitive permutation group with solvable 1-point stabilizer H. A case by case analysis, supported by some
technical results established in Proposition 7.20, leaves only a few sporadic possibilities for Γ with p = 3;
see Proposition 7.21. However, a direct computation shows that none of them provides a counterexample.
Therefore, Theorem 7.22 holds and Theorem 1.1 follows whenever (***) fails and Γ is primitive on ∆.
As we have already mentioned, the investigation of the case where Γ is imprimitive on ∆appears to be
much more difficult and it requires a dynamic interaction between Group theory, Function field theory and
Combinatorics. This is apparent in the quite long proof of Lemma 7.25 which allows us to assume that Γ
is a non-abelian simple group and that Case (iia) in Proposition 7.10 occurs, i.e. Λ1 = ∆0 ∪{P} is a block

Section 8 exhibits curves that hit the bounds in Theorems 1.1 and 1.3. We also point out in Proposition
8.4 that a large automorphism group Aut(X) with respect to (1), say |Aut(X)| ≈cγ(X)4, may not be large
compared to the genus as being |Aut(X)| ≈cg(X).
2. Background and basic results from Algebraic Geometry
In this paper, X stands for a (projective, geometrically irreducible, non-singular) algebraic curve of genus
g(X) ≥2 defined over an algebraically closed field K of positive characteristic p > 0. Furthermore, Aut(X)
denotes the automorphism group of X fixing K elementwise.
For a subgroup G of Aut(X), let ¯
X denote a non-singular model of K(X)G, that is, an algebraic curve
with function field K(X)G, where K(X)G consists of all elements of K(X) fixed by every element in G. As
customary, ¯
X is called the quotient curve of X by G and denoted by X/G. The field extension K(X)|K(X)G
is Galois of degree |G|.
Since our approach is mostly group theoretical, we prefer to use terminology from Group theory as in
[39, 21, 22, 24]. For Algebraic geometry and Function fields, our reference are [39, 64].
Let Φ be the Galois cover of X →¯

P ∈X is a ramification point of G if the stabilizer GP of P in G is non-trivial; the ramification index eP
is |GP |; a point ¯Q ∈¯
X is a branch point of G if there is a ramification point P ∈X such that Φ(P) = ¯Q;
the ramification (branch) locus of G is the set of all ramification (branch) points. The G-orbit of P ∈X is
the subset of X o = {R | R = g(P), g ∈G}, and it is long if |o| = |G|, otherwise o is short. For a point ¯Q,
the G-orbit o lying over ¯Q consists of all points P ∈X such that Φ(P) = ¯Q. For P ∈o, the Orbit theorem
states that |o| = |G|/|GP |; hence ¯Q is a branch point if and only if o is a short G-orbit. It may be that G has
no short orbits. This is the case if and only if every non-trivial element in G is fixed–point-free on X, that
is, the cover Φ is unramified. On the other hand, G has a finite number of short orbits. For a non-negative
integer i, the i-th ramification group of X at P is denoted by G(i)

A subgroup of Aut(X) is a p′-group (or a prime to p group) if its order is prime to p. A subgroup G of
Aut(X) is tame if the 1-point stabilizer of any point in G is a p′-group. Otherwise, G is non-tame (or wild).
Obviously, every p′-subgroup of Aut(X) is tame, but the converse is not always true. From the classical
Hurwitz’s bound, if |G| > 84(g(X) −1) then G is non-tame; see [63] or [39, Theorems 11.56]. An orbit o
of G is tame if GP is a p′-group for P ∈o, otherwise o is a non-tame orbit of G. The following result due
to Stichtenoth is on the number of short orbits of large automorphism groups [63]; see also Theorems 11.56
and 11.116 in [39].
Result 2.2. If |G| > 4(g(X) −1) then the quotient curve X/G is rational. If p > 2 and G has odd order,
then |G| ≤15(g(X) −1). If the order of G exceeds 84(g(X) −1), then G has either
(a) exactly three short orbits, two tame and one non-tame, and |G| < 24g(X)2, or
(b) exactly two short orbits, both non-tame, and |G| < 16g(X)2, or
(c) only one short orbit which is non-tame, or
(d) exactly two short orbits, one tame and one non-tame.
The p-rank of X is defined to be the rank of the (elementary abelian) group of the p-torsion points in the
Jacobian variety of X, and it coincides with the Hasse-Witt invariant of X. The p-rank of X is denoted by
γ(X). Then 0 ≤γ(X) ≤g(X), and X is an ordinary curve if γ(X) = g(X). Let ¯
X = X/S for a p-subgroup
S of Aut(X). The Deuring-Shafarevich formula, see [67] or [39, Theorem 11.62], states that

For automorphism groups with special structures or actions on X, the classical Hurwitz bound can be

Result 2.5. Let g(X) ≥2.
(i) If G is abelian then |G| ≤4g(X) + 4.
(ii) If G fixes a point P of X and its order is prime to p then |G| ≤4g(X) + 2.
Result 2.6. If X is a rational curve, then Aut(X) ∼= PGL(2, K).
The automorphism groups of elliptic and hyperelliptic curves are well known, see [61], [39, Theorem 11.94]
and [39, Theorem 11.98]. For the following results concerning the 1-point stabilizer of the automorphism
group of an elliptic curve; see [61, Theorem 10.1], [39, Theorem 11.94], [39, Examples 11.96 and 11.97]. Here
we add that if p = 2 and E is not supersingular then the Sylow 2-subgroup of Aut(E)P has order 2. In fact,
if S2 was a subgroup of order 4 in Aut(E)P , then the Deuring-Shafarevich formula applied to S2 would yield
0 = γ(E) −1 = 4(−1) + 4 −1 + d with an even integer d, a contradiction.
Result 2.7. Let E be an elliptic curve over K. For any point P of E, one of the following holds.
• Aut(E)P ∼= C2, C3, C4, C6;
• Aut(E)P ∼= C3 ⋊C4, p = 3 and j(E) = 0;
• Aut(E)P ∼= SL(2, 3), p = 2 and j(E) = 0.
Result 2.8. Let G be an automorphism group of an elliptic curve E over K. If P is any point of E and GP
is abelian then GP is cyclic and |GP | ∈{1, 2, 4, 6}.
Proof. From Result 2.7, the order of GP divides 6 unless either p = 2 and |GP | = 24, or p = 3 and |GP | = 12.
In the exceptional cases, GP is not abelian. In fact, for p = 2 the Sylow 2-subgroup of Aut(E)P is isomorphic
to the quaternion group of order 8 by (iii) [39, Theorem 11.94], whereas, for p = 3, Aut(E)P ̸∼= C3 × C4. To
show the latter claim, consider the quotient curve ¯E = E/⟨ι⟩where ι is the elliptic involution of Aut(E)P .
Since ι centralizes Aut(E)P , we have Aut( ¯E) ¯
P ∼= (C3 ⋊C4)/⟨ι⟩. Furthermore, ¯E is rational. Since Aut( ¯E) ¯
P
has order 6, this implies that Aut( ¯E) ¯
P is not abelian. Therefore, Aut(E)P ̸∼= C3 × C4.
□
Automorphisms of curves of prime order are studied in [40]; see also [39, Theorem 11.108] and [1]. Here
we quote the following claim.
Result 2.9. If a prime number n other than p is the order of an automorphism of X then n ≤2g(X) + 1.
Non-tame abelian groups fixing a point are studied by Serre [59]; see also [39, Lemma 11.77, 11.75].

3. Background from Group Theory and Number Theory
Definitions and results from Group theory which play a role in the proofs are quoted below.
Our notation and terminology comes from [74]; see also [29, 41, 31, 73]. In particular, extensions of groups
are written in the following ways: A×B denotes the direct product, with normal subgroups A, B; also A⋊B
denotes a semidirect product (or split extension) with a normal subgroup A and a subgroup B; and A · B
denotes a non.split extension with a normal subgroup A and quotient B, but no subgroup B; finally A.B
or AB denotes an unspecified extension. Moreover, O(G) stands for the largest normal subgroup of a finite
group G of odd order. Furthermore, q denotes a power of a prime.
The finite subgroups of PGL(2, K) were classified by Dickson; see [65] and also Theorem A.8 in [39].


## Page 9


LARGE AUTOMORPHISM GROUPS COMPARED TO THE p-RANK OF ALGEBRAIC CURVES IN CHARACTERISTIC p 9
Result 3.1 (Dickson’s classification of finite subgroups of the projective linear group PGL(2, K)). Any finite
subgroup of the group PGL(2, K) is isomorphic to a subgroup of PGL(2, q) for some q = ph. The subgroups
of PGL(2, q) are listed below.
(i) cyclic group whose order divides q ± 1;
(ii) elementary abelian p-group whose order divides q;
(iii) dihedral groups whose order divides 2(q ± 1);
(iv) the alternating group Alt4, for p > 2, or p = 2 and h even;
(v) the symmetric group Sym4, for p > 2;
(vi) the alternating group Alt5, q2 ≡1 (mod 5);
(vii) the semidirect product of an elementary abelian p-group of order pa, a ≤h, by a cyclic group of order
n > 1 with n | (pa −1);
(viii) PSL(2, pf), f | h;
(ix) PGL(2, pf), f | h.
In particular, no non-trivial element of PGL(2, K) fixes more than two points. For q > 11, the minimal size
of a set on which PSL(2, q) can act transitively is q + 1; see [41, Kapitel II, Satz 8.28].
The maximal subgroups of PSU(3, q) for q odd were classified by Mitchell [52]; see also [54] and [39,
Theorem A.10]. In this paper we only need the following corollaries of Mitchell’s classification; see [52,
Theorem 30].
Result 3.2. Let µ = gcd(3, q + 1). The subgroups of PSU(3, q) of order q3(q2 −1)/µ are maximal. For
q > 5, they are the largest subgroups of PSU(3, q). For q = 5 the largest subgroups of PSU(3, q) have order
2520 and are isomorphic to Alt7. Let Ωbe a set of smallest size on which PSU(3, q) may have a non-trivial
action. Then |Ω| ≥q3 + 1 with an exception for q = 5 and |Ω| = 60.
A group G is of (group-theoretic) 2-rank h if h is the maximum rank of an abelian 2-subgroup of G.
2-groups with a cyclic subgroup of index 2 were classified by Zassenhaus; see [31, Theorem 12.5.1], or [41,
Kapitel I, Satz 14.9], or [76],
Result 3.3 (Classification 2-groups with a cyclic subgroup of index 2). The non-cyclic groups of order 2n
which contain a cyclic subgroup of index 2 are generated by two elements a, b and are of the following types:
• a2n−1 = 1, b2 = 1, ba = ab (direct product of two cyclic groups);
• n ≥3, a2n−1 = 1, b2 = a2n−2, ba = a−1b (generalized quaternion group);
• n ≥2, a2n−1 = 1, b2 = 1, ba = a−1b (dihedral group);
• n ≥4, a2n−1 = 1, b2 = 1, ba = a−1+2n−2b (semidihedral - also called quasidihedral - group);
• n ≥4, a2n−1 = 1, b2 = 1, ba = a1+2n−2b (modular maximal-cyclic group, that is type (3) group with
notation from [41]).
Observe that the dihedral group for n = 2 is an elementary abelian group of order 4. Some elementary
properties of the above 2-groups are as follows; see [41, Kapitel I, Satz 14.9]. A generalized quaternion group
has rank 1 as it contains a unique involution, the other groups listed in Result 3.3 have rank 2 as they contain
an elementary abelian group of order 4 but no elementary abelian group of order 8. Generalized quaternion
groups, as well as dihedral and semidihedral groups, have center of order 2. Dihedral, semidihedral and
modular maximal-cyclic groups have non-central involutions. The semidihedral group of order 2n has as
many as 2n−2 + 1 involutions.
The quotient groups of a generalized quaternion group, as well as of a
semidihedral group, with respect to its center is a dihedral group. A modular maximal-cyclic group has
exactly three involutions and four elements of order 4. In particular, it contains neither a dihedral group of
order 2n ≥8 nor a generalized quaternion group.
Result 3.4 (The Schur-Zassenhaus theorem; see Kapitel I, Hauptsatz 18.1 in [41]). Let N be a normal
subgroup of G such that |N| and [G : N] are coprime. Then N has a complement in G, that is, G = N ⋊U


## Page 10


10
MASSIMO GIULIETTI, G´ABOR KORCHM´AROS, AND MARCO TIMPANELLA
for a subgroup U of G of order [G : N]. If G has a cyclic Sylow 2-subgroup then G has a normal subgroup
N of odd order.
A representation of a group G over a vector space V is completely irreducible if every G-invariant subspace
W has a G-invariant complement W ′, that is, V = W ⊕W ′.
Result 3.5 (The Maschke theorem; see Kapitel I, Satz 17.7 in [41]). A representation of a finite group over
a field whose characteristic is prime to the order of the group is completely reducible.
Result 3.6. Let G be a perfect group with a non-trivial subgroup M contained in the center of G, that is,
G is a central extension of ¯G = G/M. If ¯G ∼= PSL(2, q) then G ∼= SL(2, q) with |M| = 2 and q odd, unless
q = 9 and |M| ∈{3, 4, 6}. If ¯G ∼= PSU(3, q) then G = SU(3, q) with q ≡−1 (mod 3) and |M| = 3. If
¯G ∼= Sz(q) then q = 8 and one of two cases occurs, according as |M| = 2, or M ∼= C2 ×C2. For ¯G ∼= Ree(q),
no such central extension exists.
Result 3.7 (The Burnside-Thompson theorem [70]). Let G be a Frobenius group with Frobenius kernel F
and complement C. Then the Sylow subgroups of C are cyclic or generalized quaternion groups, and F is a
nilpotent characteristic subgroup of G.
Result 3.8 (The Gorenstein-Walter theorem [26, 27]; see also Chapter 16 in [29]). Let G be a group with a
dihedral Sylow 2-subgroup S2. Then, either G = O(G) ⋊S2, or G/O(G) ∼= Alt7, or G/O(G) is isomorphic
to a subgroup of PΓL(2, q) containing PSL(2, q).
Result 3.9 (The Brauer-Suzuki theorem [11]; see also Chapter 12 in [29]). Let G be a group with a generalized
quaternion Sylow 2-subgroup. Then the center of G/O(G) has order 2.
For q odd, a Sylow 2-subgroup of SL(2, q) is a generalized quaternion group.
Result 3.10 (The Alperin-Brauer-Gorenstein theorem [4]). Let G be a non-abelian simple group containing
a semidihedral Sylow 2-subgroup S2. Then, either G ∼= PSL(3, q) with q ≡3 (mod 4), or G ∼= PSU(3, q) with
q ≡1 (mod 4) where q is an odd prime power, or G is isomorphic to the Mathieu group M11.
Result 3.11 (The Alperin theorem [3]). A Sylow 2-subgroup of a non-abelian simple group with 2-rank equal
to 2 is either dihedral, or semidihedral, or wreathed, or isomorphic to a Sylow 2-subgroup of SU(3, 2).
Result 3.12. Any non-abelian simple group of (group theoretic) 2-rank equal to 2 is isomorphic to one of
the following groups: PSL(2, q), q ≥5, PSL(3, q), PSU(3, q), PSU(3, 4), Alt7, M11 where q is a power of an
odd prime. The Ree group Ree(q) with q a power of 3 has an elementary abelian Sylow 2-subgroup of order
8, and its (group theoretic) 2-rank is equal to 3.
Hering [35] partly relying on previous work by E. Shult [68] proved the following classification theorem.
Result 3.13 (Hering’s classification of transitive group spaces). Let G be a group acting transitively (but
not necessarily faithfully) on a set Ωwith |Ω| > 2. Assume that for some P ∈Ωthe stabilizer GP contains
a normal d-subgroup Q which is sharply transitive on Ω\ {P}. If S is the normal closure of Q in G, then S
induces a 2-transitive permutation group on Ωand one of the following cases occurs:
(i) S ∼= PSL(2, q), SL(2, q), Sz(q), PSU(3, q), SU(3, q), Ree(q), where q is a power of d, and |Ω| equals
q + 1 in the linear case, q2 + 1 in the Suzuki case and q3 + 1 in the unitary and Ree case.
(ii) S ∼= PΓL(2, 8) and |Ω| = 28.
(iii) S is a sharply 2-transitive permutation group on Ω.
(iv) |Ω| = u2 for u ∈{3, 5, 7, 11, 23, 29, 59}, S = Ou(S) ⋊Q, Ou(S) is extraspecial of order u3 and expo-
nent u, Z(Ou(S)) = Z(S) is the kernel of the action of S on Ω, and S induces a sharply 2-transitive
permutation group on Ω.
If |Ω| −1 is a power of a prime d, then any Sylow d-subgroup Sd fixes exactly one point and no non-
trivial element of Sd fixes more than one point. In particular, any two Sylow d-subgroups of S have trivial
intersection.

LARGE AUTOMORPHISM GROUPS COMPARED TO THE p-RANK OF ALGEBRAIC CURVES IN CHARACTERISTIC p11
Each of the groups S in Result 3.13 is called a He-group of degree |Ω|.
For |Ω| odd, the hypothesis on the sharply-transitivity of Q on Ωcan be weakened by requiring the
semi-regularity of Q on Ω; [35, 68]:
Result 3.14. Let G be a group acting transitively (but not necessarily faithfully) on a set Ω. Suppose that the
stabilizer GP with P ∈Ωhas a normal subgroup Q of even order that is semiregular on Ω. Then the normal
closure of Q in G either has a transitive normal subgroup of odd order and Q is a Frobenius complement,
or acts 2-transitively on Ωas one of the groups PSL(2, 2e), Sz(2e) or PSU(3, 2e) (for some e), in its usual
2-transitive representation.
Since groups of odd order are solvable, a direct corollary of this theorem is the following.
Result 3.15. Let N be non-abelian simple group acting transitively on a set ∆. Suppose that the stabilizer
NP with P ∈∆has a normal subgroup UP of even order that is semiregular on ∆. Then N acts 2-transitively
on ∆.
Result 3.16. (Holt, [38, Main Theorem]) Let G be a finite 2-transitive permutation group of even degree,
and suppose that the 1-point stabilizer of G is solvable. Then either G has a regular normal subgroup, or
G has a normal 2-transitive subgroup W isomorphic to PSL(2, q), PSU(3, q) (for some odd prime power q),
or to Ree(q). In the latter case, the action of W is the natural 2-transitive permutation representation of
PSL(2, q), PSU(3, q) and Ree(q) respectively, with only one exception: G ∼= PΓL(2, 8) and W ∼= PSL(3, 2) ∼=
PSL(2, 8) with degree 28.
Result 3.17. (O’Nan, [54, Theorem B]) Let G be a finite 2-transitive group of odd degree, and suppose that
the 1-point stabilizer G has an abelian normal subgroup of order > 1. Then G has either a regular normal
subgroup, or a normal 2-transitive subgroup W isomorphic to
(i) PSL(r + 1, q), with 1 + q + . . . + qr odd and r ≥1, or
(ii) PSU(3, 2k), or
(iii) Sz(22k+1),
and the action of W is the natural 2-transitive representation of PSL(r + 1, q), PSU(3, 2k) and Sz(22k+1),
respectively.
The following result is a corollary of the classification of finite 2-transitive permutation groups.
Result 3.18. Let G be a non-abelian simple 2-transitive permutation group of degree n. Suppose that either
n = uh + 1 or n = 2uh + 1 with u prime and h ≥2. Then one of the following cases occurs
(i) G ∼= Altn, n ≥5, n ̸= 6.
(ii) G ∼= PSL(2, q), n = q + 1 = uh + 1, q ≥4.
(iii) G ∼= PSU(3, q), n = q3 + 1 = uh + 1, q ≥3.
(iv) G = PSp(2d, 2), n = 22d−1 ± 2d−1 = uh + 1.
(iv) G ∼= Sz(q), n = q2 + 1 = 2h + 1, q ≥8.
(v) G ∼= Ree(q), n = q3 + 1 = uh + 1, u = 3, h ≥3.
Proof. The list of all non-abelian simple 2-transitive permutation groups is found, for instance, in [14]. A
direct inspection shows that there is enough to rule out the case G ∼= PSL(d, q) for d ≥3. In this case, n =
1+q+q2+. . .+qd−1, and comparison with the extra-condition on n shows that either q(1+q+. . .+qd−2) = uh,
or q(1 + q + . . . + qd−2) = 2uh. Since (q, 1 + . . . + qd−2) = 1, the former case cannot occur whereas, in the
latter case, q = 2 and 1 + 2 + . . . + 2d−2 = uh whence 2d−1 + 1 = uh. From this h = 1; see Result 3.41, a
contradiction.
□
Result 3.19. (Huppert, [42, Theorem 7.3]) Any double transitive solvable permutation group on ∆is iso-
morphic to a subgroup of H of AΓL(1, dr) for a prime d, and acts on ∆as H on the elements of the finite
field Fdr, except when dr ∈{32, 52, 72, 112, 232, 34}.

Result 3.20. (Gleason, [35, Lemma 2]) Let G be a permutations group on a finite set ∆such that, for some
prime d and each P ∈∆, there exists an element of G of order d which fixes P but has no other fixed points.
Then G is transitive on ∆.
Result 3.21. ([73, Theorem 12.2]) Any normal subgroup of a primitive permutation group is transitive.
Result 3.22. ([34, Hilfssatz 6]) Let G be a transitive permutation group on a set ∆such that each involution
has exactly 2 fixed points. Then a Sylow 2-subgroup of G is either dihedral or semi-dihedral, unless G has a
subgroup F of index 2, and |∆| ≡2 (mod 4). In the exceptional case, ∆splits into two F-orbits of the same
length, say ∆1 and ∆2, so that each involution of F has exactly one fixed point in ∆1 (and one in ∆2). The
action on ∆1 and the structure of F is determined by Result 3.14.
The following necessary and sufficient condition for a transitive group to be primitive is a classical result,
see [73, Theorem 7.4]. The second claim is shown in the proof of [73, Theorem 7.4].
Result 3.23. Let G be a transitive permutations group on a finite set ∆. Then G is imprimitive on ∆
if and only if, for any P ∈∆, the stabilizer GP of P in G has a strongly intermediate subgroup Z, i.e.
GP ⫋Z ⫋G. If this is the case, then the Z-orbit of P is a block of G.
Result 3.24. ([73, Theorem 7.5]) Let G be an imprimitive transitive permutations group on a finite set ∆.
For any P ∈∆, the lattice of intermediate subgroups between GP and G is isomorphic to the lattice of blocks
of G containing P.

Occasionally, we also need some elementary, apparently unquoted results from Group theory and Number
theory.
Result 3.28. Let A, B be two subgroups of G such that A is a normal subgroup of G and AB = A ⋊B. If
every element in B commutes with every element in A, then AB = A × B. If A ⋊B = A × B and A is a
p-group while B is a prime to p group, then B comprises all elements of A × B whose orders are prime to p.


## Page 13


LARGE AUTOMORPHISM GROUPS COMPARED TO THE p-RANK OF ALGEBRAIC CURVES IN CHARACTERISTIC p13
In fact, if every element in B commutes with every element in A, implies that B is also a normal subgroup
of AB. Since A and B intersect trivially, AB is the direct product of A and B. The last claims follows from
the fact that if ab = ba for two elements a, b in a group, then the order of ab is equal to smallest common
multiple of the orders of a and b.
Result 3.29. Let S be a generalized quaternion group. If M be a proper normal subgroup of S such that
the factor group S/M is an elementary abelian group, then S/M has order either two or four.
Proof. Let N = Z(S). Then |N| = 2 and N ≤M. Moreover,
S/M ∼= S/N
M/N
Since S/N is a dihedral group, this shows that S/M is also a dihedral group. Therefore, S/M is elementary
abelian if and only if either |S/M| = 2, or |S/M| = 4.
□
The following result is [75, Lemma 5].
Result 3.30. Let U be a solvable group with a semidihedral Sylow 2-subgroup S. Then either U has a normal
2-complement or |S| = 16 and U has a normal subgroup N of odd order such that U/N is isomorphic with
GL(2, 3).
A group G containing an index 2 subgroup T is generalized dicyclic with respect to T, if each element in
the coset G \ T is an involution.
Result 3.31. If G is a generalized dicyclic group with respect to T, then T is abelian.
Proof. Take u ∈T and v ∈G \ T. Then vu ∈G \ T. Therefore, vu has order 2 whence vuv = u−1 and
vu−1v = u. If, in addition, w ∈G \ T then wvuvw = wu−1w = u whence (wv)u = u(wv). Therefore, wv
commutes with u. Since each element in T is of the form wv with w, v ∈G \ T, the claim follows.
□
Result 3.32. Let H = H1.H2. · · · .Hk. If H has a cyclic Sylow 2-subgroup then the Sylow 2-subgroups of
the factors Hi are also cyclic.
Proof. Let 1 = A0  A1  A2  · · ·  Ak = H be a subnormal series of H corresponding to the extension
H = H1.H2. · · · .Hk. Then Hj = Aj+1/Aj with j = 0, . . . k −1 are the factors. If a Sylow 2-subgroup of H
is a cyclic then all Ai has a cyclic Sylow 2-subgroup and hence the factors Hi also have this property.
□
We will use the following corollary of Result 3.32.
Result 3.33. Let H = H1.H2. · · · .Hk. For some 1 ≤i ≤k, let Hi be isomorphic to one of the follow-
ing groups Alt4, Sym4, Q8, C2 × C2, S3 × S3, GL(2, 3), SL(2, 3), SU(3, 2), PSL(2, 3), PSU(3, 2), and the central
products C4 ⊙GL(2, 3) and SL(2, 3) ⊙SL(2, 3). Then Sylow 2-subgroups of H are not cyclic.
Result 3.34. Let U = U0.U1 with a cyclic subgroup U0. Let M be a normal subgroup of U whose order is
a power of a prime d. If d ∤|U1| then M is contained in U0. In particular, M is cyclic.
Proof. From the first homomorphism theorem, the factor group MU0/U0 is isomorphic to a subgroup of U1.
Since d ∤|U1|, the Lagrange theorem yields that MU0/U0 has order 1, i.e. MU0 and hence M is contained
in U0.
□
Result 3.35. Assume that a group U has a central involution u such that ¯U = U/⟨u⟩. Let U = U1.U2. · · · .Uk.
If a Sylow 2-subgroup of ¯U is cyclic then the Sylow 2-subgroups of Ui are abelian.
Proof. Let S2 be a Sylow 2-subgroup of U.
Then u is a central involution of S2 and the factor group

contradiction. Therefore the G-orbit V of v in V (h, p) is not trivial. Let E be the set of vectors in V which
are fixed by Sp. From the last claim in Result 3.13, any two Sylow p-subgroups of G have trivial intersection.

subgroups of G. Then |V| = (n + 1)|E|. Moreover, let D be the subgroup of C which preserves V. Since
D centralizes Sp, E is left invariant by D. Since C is semiregular on E, no non-trivial element of D fixes a
vector in E. Therefore, |D| ≤|E|. Furthermore, since G is a normal subgroup of ⟨G, C⟩= GC, the G-orbits
form a C-invariant partition of the non-zero vectors of V (h, p). Then the images of V under the action of C

Proposition 4.4. Let ˜
X be a curve defined over an algebraically closed field of characteristic p. Let ˜Γ be a
subgroup of Aut( ˜
X) with a non-tame orbit ∆, |∆| > 2, such that
(i) ˜Γ is doubly transitive on ∆,
(ii) the kernel of the action of ˜Γ on ∆is trivial.
Then either ˜Γ is solvable, or ˜Γ acts on ∆as one of the following groups in its natural 2-transitive permutation
representation where q is a power of p.
(A) ˜Γ ∼= PSL(2, q) with |∆| = q + 1, and q ≥4;
(B) ˜Γ ∼= PGL(2, q) with |∆| = q + 1 and q ≥5 odd;
(C) ˜Γ ∼= PSU(3, q) with |∆| = q3 + 1, and q ≥3;
(D) ˜Γ ∼= PGU(3, q) with |∆| = q3 + 1 and q ≡−1 (mod 3), q ≥3;


## Page 19


LARGE AUTOMORPHISM GROUPS COMPARED TO THE p-RANK OF ALGEBRAIC CURVES IN CHARACTERISTIC p19
(E) ˜Γ ∼= Sz(q) with q = 2q2
0, q0 = 2s, s ≥2, and |∆| = q2 + 1;
(F) ˜Γ ∼= Ree(q) with q = 3q2
0, q0 = 3s, s ≥3, and |∆| = q3 + 1;
(G) ˜Γ ∼= PΓL(2, 8) = Ree(3) and |∆| = 28.
Proof. From (i) and (ii), ˜Γ can be viewed as a 2-transitive permutation group on ∆. Thus, ˜Γ has trivial
center, and the (unique) minimal normal subgroup W of ˜Γ is either non-solvable or an elementary abelian
group. Since 2-transitivity implies primitivity, Result 3.21 yields that W is transitive on ∆. From Result
2.1, the 1-point stabilizer of P in ˜Γ is solvable. Therefore, if W is elementary abelian then ˜Γ is solvable. In
particular, if ˜Γ has a regular normal subgroup, then ˜Γ is solvable.
We may assume that W and hence ˜Γ are non-solvable.
If |∆| is even, Result 3.16 shows that W is
isomorphic to either PSL(2, q), or PSU(3, q), or Ree(q), and W also satisfies Conditions (i) and (ii), or
W ∼= PSL(2, 8) and |∆| = 28. For the case where |∆| is odd, we point out that the hypotheses of Result 3.17
are satisfied. If p divides the order of the 1-point stabilizer ˜ΓP of ˜Γ, then the center of the Sylow p-subgroup
of ˜ΓP is an abelian characteristic subgroup of ˜SP and hence it is an abelian normal subgroup of ˜ΓP as ˜SP
is a normal subgroup of ˜ΓP by (i) of Result 2.1. Otherwise, ˜ΓP is cyclic, and it is itself an abelian normal
subgroup of ˜ΓP . From Result 3.17, W is isomorphic to either PSL(r + 1, q), or PSU(3, q), or Sz(q) for some
q. The case (i) in Result 3.17 only occurs for r = 1 since the 1-point stabilizer of PSL(r + 1, q) for r > 1 is
isomorphic to AGL(2, q) and hence it is not solvable with an exception for r = 2, q = 3. However, AGL(2, 3)
has a non-cyclic Sylow 2-subgroup, and hence the exceptional case cannot actually occur either. Thus in
case (i), q is even and W ∼= PSL(2, q).
From the above discussion, ˜Γ is isomorphic to a subgroup of Aut(W) containing W where W is isomorphic
to one of the simple groups PSL(2, q), PSU(3, q), Sz(q), Ree(q). We investigate Aut(W) case by case.
4.1. W ∼= PSL(2, q), |∆| = q + 1. In this case, PSL(2, q) ≤˜Γ ≤PΓL(2, q) up to an isomorphism. We may
apply Proposition 3.36 for d = p since the one-point stabilizer of PSL(2, q) contains no normal subgroup of
order different from a power of d, and hence Result 2.1 implies d = p. Therefore, Proposition 3.36 together
with Result 2.1 show the claim.
4.2. W ∼= PSU(3, q), |∆| = q3 + 1. In this case PSU(3, q) ≤˜Γ ≤PΓU(3, q) up to an isomorphism. where
q = dr with a prime d. As in 4.1, d = p, since the one-point stabilizer of PSU(3, q) contains no normal
subgroup of order different from a power of d. In its unique doubly transitive representation, PΓU(3, q) is
the semilinear group on the set Hq(Fq2) of Fq2-rational points of the Hermitian curve Hq of homogeneous
equation Y qZ + Y Zq −Xq+1 = 0. For q odd, let C be the conic of homogeneous equation Y Z −1

while for q even take for C the line of equation X = 0. First, the odd order case is investigated. In PG(2, q2),

Remark 4.7. Let p = 2. Then the Fermat curve H2 of homogeneous equation X3 + Y 3 + Z3 = 0 provides
an example for cases (L’) and (L”) as Aut(H2) has a subgroup isomorphic to PGU(3, 2) that acts on the nine

has a subgroup isomorphic to AGL(1, 3) ∼= Sym3, Case (J’) with u = 3 occurs for H2. Furthermore, Case
(J’) also occurs for u = 5, in fact the elliptic curve (the Suzuki curve S2 formally defined for q0 = 1) of
homogeneous equation X3 +XZ2 +Y 2Z +Y Z2 = 0 has subgroup in Aut(S2) that is isomorphic to C5 ⋊C4.
In these examples γ(X) = 0. However, in Case (L), ˜Γ has an involution with more than one fixed points,
and hence γ(X) ̸= 0 by Result 2.13.

with a Γ-orbit ∆and a point P ∈∆such that the Sylow p-subgroup SP of ΓP is not trivial. We assume
that Γ does not fix P.

Proposition 5.5. Assume that (*) and (**) hold, but (***) does not hold.. If p = 2 and g(X) = 3 then
Aut(X) has no subgroup isomorphic to PSL(2, 3).
Proof. Take two points P, Q ∈∆such that both SP and SQ satisfy (*) but |SP ∩SQ| = 1. If X is not
hyperelliptic, then it has a non-singular plane model given by a quartic curve C. From (*), P and Q are
distinct simple Galois points of C. However, since p = 2 and g(X) > 1, [48, Theorem 1.1] shows that no such
curve exists. For hyperelliptic X, the non-existence follows from [15, Theorem].
□
Proposition 5.6. Assume that (*) holds. If g(X) ≥2 and γ(X) = 1, then |SP | = p = 2, the Aut(X)-orbit
containing P has size 2, and X is a hyperelliptic curve. Moreover, if ι is its hyperelliptic involution, then
Aut(X)/⟨ι⟩is either a dihedral group of order 2m, or a cyclic group of order m where m is an odd integer.
Proof. Let S be a Sylow p-subgroup containing SP . The Deuring-Shafarevic formula applied to SP shows
that p = 2, |SP | = 2, and that SP fixes a unique point Q other than P. Therefore, the claim follows from
the proof of (iii) of Lemma 5.4.
□
Remark 5.7. For q = 2, the family of curves in Example 4 shows that no restriction on the amplitude of
m and of g(X) is possible in Proposition 5.6.
Take a point Q ∈∆\ {P} and let N = SP ∩SQ. Observe that if (***) is not satisfied, then N is trivial
for some point Q ∈∆. From the first claim of Lemma 5.1, however, if |N| = 1 for all points of X other
than P, then hypothesis (*) implies that hypothesis (**) does not hold. Let x, y ∈K(X) such that K(x)

x, and Q is a zero of y. Actually, P is the unique zero of x. In fact, let ¯P be the place of K(x) lying under
P in the cover K(X)|K(x). Since this cover has degree q = |SP | and it is fully ramified at P, we have that
vP (x) = qv ¯
P (x) = q. From [K(X) : K(x)] = q, the claim follows.

U of ⟨SP , SQ⟩whose fixed field is K(x, y). Observe that U is a p-subgroup as K(X) ⊃K(x, y) ⊃K(x) and
[K(X) : K(x)] = |SP | = q, and that U must be contained in SP ∩SQ = N. This proves that the fixed field
of N is contained in K(x, y) whence the first claim follows. The Castelnuovo-Riemann inequality, see [64,
Theorem III.10.4], yields the second claim.
□

conjugacy in K, we may assume U ≤C. In fact, C contains a Sylow d-subgroup of K as |C| = |K|/|M|
implies that d divides |C|. Then m ∈CM(U) = CM(u), and hence CM(U) is a non-trivial subgroup of M.
We show first that CM(U) is a non-trivial normal subgroup of Γ. Let ¯Γ = Γ/M, ¯K = K/M ∼= C, and
¯U = UM/M ∼= U. Since C is a cyclic group by Result 2.1, U is the unique subgroup of C of order d, and
hence ¯U is the unique subgroup of ¯K of order d. Therefore, ¯U is a characteristic subgroup of ¯K. Hence, ¯U is
a normal subgroup of ¯Γ. This shows that if g ∈Γ and ¯g = gM then ¯g¯u¯g−1 = ¯uk for some integer k whence

Propositions 6.12 and 6.13 show that, in some special cases, ˜Γ = Γ/K is determined by the structure of

Proposition 6.12. Assume that both (*) and (***) hold and that SP is cyclic. If g(X) ≥2, |∆| > 2 and
SP is transitive on ∆\ {P}, then Γ is solvable, and hence either (H’), or (J’) holds for ˜Γ = Γ/K.
Proof. From the transitive action of SP on ∆\{P}, Γ is 2-transitive on ∆and hence Proposition 4.6 applies
to ˜Γ. Assume on the contrary that Γ is not solvable. Since SP is cyclic, Propositions 4.4 and 4.6 yield either
˜Γ ∼= PSL(2, p), or ˜Γ ∼= PGL(2, p) where p ≥5 and |∆| = p + 1.
For a possible counter-example to Proposition 6.12, let d = |∆|. In the family of all curves violating
Proposition 6.12 with the same d > 2, collect those with the smallest p-rank, say γ ≥2. Then among those
curves of p-rank γ, take one with smallest genus g, and name it minimal counter-example. The property for
a group of being cyclic is invariant with respect to quotients. By this invariance property, the arguments
in the proof of Theorem 6.11 can be used to show that if X is a minimal counter-example, then M has no
proper normal subgroup, and hence |M| = p.
Write K = M ⋊C, and CK(M) = M × U with a subgroup U of K. From Result 3.28, U consists of all

Since CK(M) is a normal subgroup of K, U is also a normal subgroup of Γ. Now, we may assume U to
be trivial, i.e. CK(M) = M, otherwise the curve ˆ
X = X/U with ˆΓ = Γ/U is still a counter-example to
Proposition 6.12 as ˆΓ is a non-solvable group. In fact, from (17), X and ˆ
X have the same p-rank, whereas
the set of all points of ˆ
X which are fixed by some non-trivial element of ˆ
M = MU/U consists of all points
lying under those of ∆in the cover X| ˆ
X. Moreover, (*****) also holds for Γ, as |M| = p yields that M has
no non-trivial subgroup. Let G = CΓ(M), and look at the action of Γ on M. Since Aut(M) is a cyclic group
of order p −1, whereas Γ and hence ¯Γ = Γ/M is a non-solvable group, there exists a non-trivial element

Proposition 7.10. Assume that (*) and (**) but (***) hold. If |HP | ≥3, ∆is the unique non-tame orbit
of Γ, and SP has just one short orbit ∆0 other than {P}, then one of the following holds:
(i) Γ is a primitive permutation group on ∆.
(iia) {P} ∪∆0 together with its images under the action of Γ is a system of imprimitivity of Γ on ∆.
(iib) {P} ∪∆0 is not a block of Γ but it is contained in any block of Γ through P. Moreover |∆0| > 1.
Proof. If |∆0| = 1 then SP has exactly two fixed points, namely P and R where ∆0 = {R}, and SP = SR.
Take g ∈Γ such that g(P) = R, and let T = g(R). Then gSP g−1 fixes both R and T. In particular, both
SP and gSP g−1 fixes R. Since SP is the Sylow p-subgroup of ΓP , Result 2.1 yields SP = gSP g−1 whence
SP = SR = ST follows. Hence T = P and g preserves {P, R}. Now, take h ∈ΓP and let W = h(R). Then
hSRh−1 fixes W. Since SP = hSP h−1 = hSRh−1, this yields R = W. It turns out that {P, R} and its
images form a system of imprimitivity under the action of Γ on ∆. Therefore, (iia) occurs. Also, |∆0| > 1
is supposed for the rest of the proof.
Assume that Γ acts on ∆as an imprimitive permutation group.
Then Γ has a proper subgroup T
containing ΓP properly; see Result 3.23. Moreover, TP = ΓP and in particular TP contains SP . Let ∆1
denote the orbit of P in T. Then ∆1 together with its images under the action of Γ on ∆form a system of
imprimitivity; see Result 3.24.
We first investigate the case where ∆0 ∩∆1 = ∅. From Lemma 5.1, no non-trivial element of ΓP = TP
fixes a point of ∆1 other than P. Therefore, T is a Frobenius group with Frobenius complement ΓP . From
Result 3.7, SP is cyclic for p odd, and cyclic or a generalized quaternion group for p = 2. Now, look at the
action of SP on ∆0. Since |SP | > |∆0|, the (unique) subgroup U of ΓP of order p fixes ∆0 pointwise. For
R ∈∆0, let g ∈Γ take P to R. Then gSP g−1 = SR, and U ≤SP ∩SR. Therefore, the set of fixed points of
U contains both {P} ∪∆0 and g({P} ∪∆0). On the other hand {P} ∪∆0 is the set of all fixed points of U.
Thus g({P} ∪∆0) = {P} ∪∆0. It turns out that the subgroup G of Γ preserving {P} ∪∆0 acts on it as a
transitive permutation group. Therefore ΓP < G. Moreover, G < Γ as G = Γ would imply that (***) holds
for Γ. From the second claim in Result 3.23, {P} ∪∆0 is a block, that is, {P} ∪∆0 together with its images
under the action of Γ is a system of imprimitivity. Therefore (iia) holds when ∆0 ∩∆1 = ∅.
From now on, assume that ∆0 and ∆1 have a common point. Then ∆0 ⊆∆1 as ∆0 is a ΓP -orbit and
ΓP = TP whereas ∆1 is a T-orbit.
We may choose T to be minimal so that T acts on ∆1 as a primitive permutation group. Since TP = ΓP
and ∆0 is a ΓP -orbit, we have that ∆0 is a TP -orbit as well.

We collect some preliminary results on the structure of SP ; see Propositions 7.11, 7.12, 7.13 and 7.14.
Proposition 7.11. Assume that both (*) and (**) but (***) hold. If |SP | = p, ∆is the unique non-tame
orbit of Γ, and SP has a unique short orbit ∆0 other than {P}, then Γ is imprimitive on ∆.
Proof. Since ∆0 is a short orbit of SP , the stabilizer of each point in ∆0 must be non-trivial. Since ∆0 is
the unique short orbit of SP , this may only happen when ∆0 consists of a unique point. As we have shown
at the beginning of the proof of Proposition 7.10, this yields that Γ is imprimitive on ∆.
□
Proposition 7.12. If (i) in Proposition 7.10 occurs, then order of SP is not prime.
Proof. If SP has prime order then |∆0| = 1, and hence Γ is imprimitive by Proposition 7.11.
□
Proposition 7.13. In Proposition 7.10, if SP is cyclic then ∆0 together with its images φ(∆0) under the
action of Γ form a system of imprimitivity.
Proof. Take φ ∈Γ such that ∆0 ∩φ(∆0) is non-trivial. Let R ∈∆0 ∩φ(∆0). Since R ∈∆0, the stabilizer
of R in SP is a non-trivial subgroup S of SR of order |SP |/|∆0|. Since SP is abelian this implies that S is
the subgroup of SP which fixes ∆0 pointwise. Let Q = φ(P). Then the stabilizer of R in SQ is a non-trivial
subgroup D of SR of order |SQ|/|φ(∆0)|. It turns out that S and D have the same order and both are

Proposition 7.14. If (i) in Proposition 7.10 occurs, then the center Z(SP ) of SP has an elementary abelian
subgroup E such that E is a normal subgroup of ΓP , and that no non-trivial element of E fixes a point other
than {P}.
Proof. We show first that the center Z(SP ) of SP has an element of order p fixing no point other than
P. Let M(SP ) be the unique largest elementary abelian subgroup of the center Z(SP ) of SP . Assume on
the contrary that every element in M(SP ) fixes a point in ∆0. Since Z(SP ) is a subgroup of SP and SP
is transitive on ∆0, every element in M(SP ) fixes ∆0 pointwise. Now, we may argue as in the proof of
Proposition 7.13. Take φ ∈Γ such that ∆0 ∩φ(∆0) is non-trivial. Let R ∈∆0 ∩φ(∆0). Then the arguments
used in the proof of Proposition 7.13 shows that M(SP ) and M(SQ) have the same order and both are
subgroups of SR. Since SR has a unique subgroup M(SR) and |M(SR)| = |M(SP )| = |M(SR)|, it turns
out that M(SP ) = M(SR) whence ∆0 = φ(∆0) follows. This shows that Γ is imprimitive, a contradiction.
Therefore the subgroup F(SP ) of M(SP ) consisting of all elements fixing ∆0 pointwise is properly contained
in M(SP ). If F(SP ) is trivial, then M(SP ) may be taken for E. Assume that F(SP ) is non-trivial. Write
ΓP = SP ⋊HP again. Since M(SP ) is normalized by HP where ΓP = SP ⋊HP and M(SP ) may be viewed
as a Fp-vector space V , HP has a representation ρ on V . Then ρ(HP ) leaves the subspace W of V invariant
whose vectors are the elements of F(SP ). From Result 3.5, W has a ρ(HP )-invariant complement W ′. The
subgroup E of SP whose elements are the vectors in W ′ is a normal subgroup of HP and hence of ΓP .
□

Then their direct product Sp = S1 × S2 × · · · × Sk is a Sylow p-subgroup of N and hence of Γ. Since Γ
is primitive, Proposition 7.11 together with (40) imply |∆| ≡1 (mod p). Therefore, there exists Q ∈∆
such that Sp = SQ. We may assume Q = P. In fact, if Q is that point then there exists u ∈Γ such that
u(P) = Q. Replace Ni with u−1Niu. Then the product N1 × · · · × Nk becomes u−1(N1 × · · · × Nk)u = N,
and also Sp = S1 × · · · × Sk becomes u−1Spu. Thus Sp(Q) = Q yields (u−1Spu)(P) = P. Therefore, we may

preserves Σ1 as S1 is centralized by Ni. Therefore, S2 × · · · × Sk preserves Σ1, and hence Sp itself preserves
Σ1. Since SP acts on Σ1 as S2×· · ·×Sk does, this yields that either Σ1 = {P}, or Σ1 = {P}∪∆0. Therefore,
N2 × · · · × Nk either fixes P or preserves {P} ∪∆0. In the former case, N is elementary abelian by Result
2.1, and hence |N| = dk for a prime d, and the fact that N contains SP implies d = p. On the other hand,
|∆| ≡1 mod p. But this is impossible as ∆is an orbit of N. Thus N2 × · · · × Nk preserves {P} ∪∆0.
Replacing S1 with S2 in the above argument shows that N1 also preserves {P} ∪∆0. Therefore, N itself
preserves {P} ∪∆0. But then ∆= {P} ∪∆0, and hence (***) holds, a contradiction. Thus, N = N1, that
is, N is a non-abelian simple group.
□
Proposition 7.16. The normal simple non-abelian subgroup N of Γ in Theorem 7.15 has trivial centralizer
in Γ.
Proof. Assume on the contrary that the centralizer CΓ(N) of N in Γ is not trivial. Since CΓ(N) is a normal
subgroup of Γ, Theorem 3.21 implies that CΓ(N) is transitive on ∆. Now, take a point Q ∈∆other than P
and the points in ∆0. There exists h ∈CΓ(N) such that h(P) = Q. If s is a non-trivial element of SP then
hsh−1 fixes Q. On the other hand, s ∈N by Theorem 7.15, and hence h ∈CΓ(N) implies hsh−1 = s. But
then s also fixes Q a contradiction as SP ∩SQ = {1}.
□
Proposition 7.16 yields that N ≤Γ ≤Aut(N) up to an isomorphism. Moreover, since the socle of Γ is a
direct product of minimal normal subgroups, |CΓ(N)| = 1 implies that N is the socle of Γ.

Theorem 7.19. Let p = 2. Assume that both (*) and (**) but (***) hold, and that SP has only one short
orbit ∆0 other than {P}. If Γ is a primitive group on ∆, then |Γ| ≤400γ(X)4.
Proof. From Theorem 7.18, if |Γ| > 400γ(X)4 then N is a non-abelian simple, primitive permutation group
on ∆containing the Sylow 2-subgroups of Γ. From Proposition 7.14, there exists a normal subgroup E ≤SP
of ΓP whose non-trivial elements fix no point other than P. Since p = 2, Γ together with E satisfy the
hypotheses of Result 3.15. Since N is non-abelian simple and it is the normal closure of E in Γ, this yields
that N is a 2-transitive permutation group on ∆where the Sylow 2-subgroup of N fixes a point and acts
transitively on the remaining points of ∆. But then {P} ∪∆0 = ∆, a contradiction as (***) does not
hold.
□
Unfortunately, the case p > 2 is more involved.
Theorem 7.18 offers the chance to exploit the deep
classification of primitive groups whose 1-point stabilizer is solvable.
For this purpose, we collect some
further preliminary results.
Proposition 7.20. Let p > 2. Under the assumptions of Theorem 7.15, either (52) holds, or Γ has a
non-abelian simple subgroup N that has the following properties
(i) N is a primitive permutation group on ∆.
(ii) N is not 2-transitive on ∆.
(iii) the stabilizer NP of P ∈∆in N is a semidirect product of a Sylow p-subgroup SP with a (non-trivial)
cyclic component HP .
(iv) SP is not a cyclic group.
(v) Let NP = H1....Hk.
Then Hi, for 1 ≤i ≤k, has a cyclic Sylow 2-subgroup; in particular, it
is neither an elementary abelian 2-group of order ≥4 nor isomorphic to any of the following
groups Alt4, Sym4, Q8, C2×C2, S3×S3, GL(2, 3), SL(2, 3), SU(3, 2), PSL(2, 3), PSU(3, 2), and the cen-
tral products C4 ⊙GL(2, 3) and SL(2, 3) ⊙SL(2, 3).
(vi) If NP = N0.N1 with a cyclic subgroup N0 then p divides |N1|.
(vii) Assume that there exists a group U with a central involution u such that HP = ¯U = U/⟨u⟩. Let
U = U1. · · · .Uk. Then the Sylow 2-subgroups of Ui are abelian.
Proof. (i) comes from Theorem 7.18. If N was 2-transitive then NP would be transitive on ∆\ ∆0. Since
SP is a normal subgroup of NP this would yield that every point Q ∈∆is fixed by some non-trivial element
in SP . But this contradicts our hypothesis that (***) does not hold. Therefore, (ii) holds. Moreover, (iii)
comes from Result 2.1 while (iv) from Proposition 7.13. Finally, (v), (vii) are direct consequences of Results

Proposition 7.21. Let p > 2. There exist three non-abelian simple group N satisfying all the properties in
Proposition 7.20. They only occur for p = 3, namely in the following cases:
(I) N = Alt6 and NP = (C3 × C3) ⋊C4,
(II) N = PSU(3, 8) and NP = W ⋊C2 with a group W of order 81.
(III) N = J3 and NP = (C3 × C3).(C3 × C3 × C3) ⋊C8.

Proof. We rely on the classification of all pairs (G, H) where G is non-abelian simple group and H is a
soluble maximal subgroup of G; see [50]. Since such pairs (G, H) are exactly the primitive permutation
groups G with soluble 1-point stabilizer H we have to show that no pair (G, H) given in [50] satisfies all
conditions (ii), (iii), (iv) and (v) in Proposition 7.20.
We proceed by a careful inspection of the pairs (G, H) listed in seven tables, named Table 14, . . . , 20
in [50]. First we create a shortlist by discarding all pairs (G, H) that do not satisfy the rather shrinking
conditions (iii) and (v). Then we verify whether some pairs (G, H) in the shortlist may satisfy the other
conditions (ii),(iv) and (vi).
7.2. Table 14 in [50]. The case G = Altn is considered. Then the shortlist comprises (Alt6, (C3 ×C3)⋊C4)
and, for a prime d, (Altd, Cd ⋊C(d−1)/2). In the former case p = 3 by (vi) and (I) holds. To rule out the
latter case, let V be the normal subgroup of H which is isomorphic to SP . Since d is prime, condition (iv)
implies V ̸= Cd. Now look at the natural permutation representation of Altd on a set Λ of size d. Since
p ̸= d and V is a p-group, some element L ∈Λ is fixed by V . On the other hand V is a normal subgroup of
H acting transitively on Λ. But then V fixes Λ element-wise, a contradiction.
7.3. Table 15 in [50]. The 27 sporadic simple groups are considered. Some cases entering the shortlist
arise from the Janko groups: They are (J3, ((C3 × C3).(C3 × (C3 × C3)) ⋊C8), and (J4, C29 ⋊C28), and
(J4, C43 ⋊C14), and (J4, C37 ⋊C12). The first case is (III). The latter three cases do not satisfy either
condition (vi) or condition (iv). Others arise from the Lyons group, and from the Held group, and from the
Thompson group, from the Fisher group Fi′
24, from the McLaughlin group, and from the Baby Monster group
B, and from Fisher-Griess Monster Group M. They are precisely, (Ly, C67 ⋊C22), and (Ly, C37 ⋊C18), and

Proof. By Proposition 7.9, |HP | > 8 may be assumed. Furthermore, from the fourth claim in Proposition
7.2, ∆may be assumed to be the unique non-tame orbit of Γ.
Assume Γ be not simple, and take a minimal normal subgroup N of Γ. Either, N is simple, or a direct
product of simple groups.
Since Γ is transitive on ∆, the N-orbits in ∆are blocks. Let ∆1 be the N-orbit through P.
First the case where {P} ∪∆0 is not contained in ∆1 is investigated. Then (iia) of Proposition 7.10
holds. Let G be the subgroup of Γ preserving {P} ∪∆0. Then ∆1 is also a T-orbit with T = NGP = NΓP .
Moreover, ∆0 ∩∆1 = ∅as either |∆0| = 1, or ΓP preserves ∆1 and acts transitively on ∆0. From Proposition
5.1, no non-trivial element of ΓP = TP fixes a point of ∆1 other than P. From the proof of Proposition 7.10,
T is a Frobenius group whose Frobenius complement is ΓP . We show that its Frobenius kernel F is N. If
N ∩ΓP is trivial then N = F. Otherwise, N itself is a Frobenius group (with non-trivial complement) and
F is its Frobenius kernel. From Result 3.7, F is a characteristic subgroup of N, and hence F is a normal
subgroup of Γ. Since N is a minimal normal subgroup, F = N follows. This shows that no non-trivial
element of N fixes a point in ∆1. Moreover, N is nilpotent by Result 3.7, and hence N is an elementary
abelian group of order dr with d and r ≥1. Here p ̸= d, as ΓP has some non-trivial element of order p, and
hence |∆1| ≡1 (mod p). Furthermore, ΓP contains no element of order d. In fact, such an element u fixing
P would have another fixed point on ∆1, since |∆1| = dr. This implies d ∤|ΓP | and T = N ⋊GP . Moreover,
N and G have trivial intersection, since g ∈N ∩G implies g ∈N ∩GP whence the claim follows. Let ∆2 be
the orbit of P under the action of NG. Since (NG)P ≤ΓP = GP implies (NG)P = GP , the Orbit theorem
yields |∆2| = |N|(1 + |∆0|), and two cases arise according as ∆2 ⫋∆, or ∆2 = ∆.
In the former case, take a N-orbit ∆3 disjoint from {P} ∪∆0. Since N is a normal subgroup and ∆1 is
a long orbit of N, each N-orbit in ∆is long. Therefore, |N| = ∆3. We show that no point in ∆3 is fixed
by a non-trivial element of T = N ⋊ΓP . If the contrary holds, a subgroup U of T of prime order u fixes
a point R ∈∆3. Since |T| = |N||ΓP | with |N| and |ΓP | coprime, u divides either |N| or |ΓP |. If u divides
|N|, then u = d and N is the unique Sylow d-subgroup of T. Therefore, U ≤N, but this is impossible
as N has only long orbits in ∆. If u divides |ΓP |, we find a Sylow u-subgroup Su in ΓP so that Su has a
subgroup V of order u conjugate to U in T. Any fixed point of V is in {P} ∪∆0 ⊆∆2 by Lemma 5.1.
Therefore, any fixed point R of U is the image of a point R′ ∈∆2 by an element g ∈T. But g preserves ∆2
whereas R ̸∈∆2. This shows, indeed, that no point in ∆3 is fixed by a non-trivial element of T. Therefore,
T has an orbit of length |N||GP | contained in ∆. Now, Proposition 7.8 applies. In the first exceptional case
Γ ∼= PSL(2, 5) is simple. In the second, p = 3, and |Γ| = 48, and hence (1) holds. Therefore, Proposition 7.8

Proof. From Lemma 5.4 SP is a Sylow subgroup of Γ whose order is at least p2 by (III) of Proposition 6.4.
Therefore the classification of sporadic simple groups implies that p ∈{2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13}. Moreover, from
Result 2.1, the normalizer of a Sylow subgroup SP of Γ is as semidirect product SP ⋊C with a cyclic group
C. For p ≡1 (mod 4), Lemma 5.3 together with Result 2.16 leave only one possibility for Γ, namely is M11.
This possibility cannot actually occur, as M11 has a unique prime divisor d such that d ≡1 mod 4, namely
5, so that p = 5. But 52 does not divide |M11|, a contradiction as SP has order at least p2. Since p ̸= 2, we
are left with a few possibilities, namely p ∈{3, 7, 11}.
7.9.19. p = 11. From the classification of simple sporadic groups, the possibilities for Γ are Γ ∼= J4 and
Γ ∼= F1. Let Γ ∼= J4. Then |SP | = 113. From [74, Section 5.9, Table 5.11], the complement C of the
normalizer of SP is a non-cyclic group, as being C5 × 2S4. Let Γ ∼= F1. Then the Sylow 11-subgroup has
order 112, and the component of its normalizer is a non-cyclic group, as being C5 × 2A5; see [74, Section
5.8.5, Table 5.6].
7.9.20. p = 7. From the classification of simple sporadic groups, the possibilities for Γ(p) with |SP | = 73
are He, O′N, Fi′
24. If Γ = He then the complement in the normalizer of a Sylow 7-subgroup is a non-cyclic
group as being (C3 ⋊C2) × C3; see [74, Section 5.8.7, Table 5.8]. If Γ(p) = O′N then Γ contains a subgroup
isomorphic to PSL(3, 7). Take a Sylow 7−subgroup of PSL(3, 7), which is also a Sylow 7−subgroup of
Γ. Then the complement in the normalizer of this group is a non-cyclic group as being C2 × C2 × C3; see
[74, Section 5.9, Table 5.11]. Neither Γ = Fi′
24 occurs since, in Fi′
24, a Sylow 7-subgroup is contained in
a subgroup isomorphic to He; see [74, Section 5.7.6] so that a component of a normalizer of a Sylow 7-
subgroup of Fi′
24 is not a cyclic group. From the classification of simple sporadic groups, the possibilities for
Γ(p) with |SP | = 72 are Co1, Th = F3, BM. If Γ = Co1 then the component in the normalizer of a Sylow
7-subgroup is a non-cyclic group as being C3 ⋊2 · A4; see [74, Section 5.5.1, Table 5.3]. If Γ = Th then the
component in the normalizer of a Sylow 7-subgroup is a non-cyclic group as being C3 × 2S4; see [74, Section
5.8.7, Table 5.8]. Neither Γ = BM occurs since Th is a subgroup of BM; see [74, Section 5.8.7]. From the
classification of simple sporadic groups, the Monster is the only possibility for Γ with |SP | > 73. We are left
with the Monster F1 whose Sylow 7-subgroups have order 76. From [74, Section 5.8.5, Table 5.6], F1 has a
subgroup T = U ⋊V where |U| = 75, and V ∼= GL(2, 7). Since GL(2, 7) has a subgroup W ∼= C7 ⋊(C6 ×C6),
T contains a subgroup S ⋊(C6 × C6) where |S| = 76, and hence S ∼= SP . Therefore, a complement of the
normalizer of SP is a non-cyclic group, a contradiction to Result 2.1.
7.9.21. p = 3. First the cases Γ ∼= F1, BM, Fi23, Fi′
24 are investigated. In each of these cases, the order of
|Γ| is divisible by 313. From Proposition 7.23, |SP | < |∆0|4 may be assumed. Then 34 ≤|∆0| = 3m. Since G
is transitive on {P}+∆0, then 3m +1 divides |Γ|. On the other hand, for each integer m such that 4 ≤m ≤
20, the set Σ of primes {37, 41, 73, 61, 67, 103, 193, 547, 661, 1181, 2851, 16493, 398581, 21523361, 42521761}
contains a divisor of 3m + 1, and hence |Γ|, a contradiction as none of the sporadic simple groups has a
divisor from the set Σ.
If Γ ∼= Th, then a Sylow 3-subgroup U of Γ(p) has order 310. From Proposition 7.23, |SP | < |∆0|4 may
be assumed. This leaves just one possibility for |∆|, namely |∆0| = 33. On the other hand, from [74, Section

5.8.7 Table 5.8], Γ has a maximal subgroup V of order 310 · 24 such that V = W ⋊S where |W| = 39 and

If Γ ∼= HN, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 36. From [74, Section 5.8.7 Table 5.8], Γ has a
maximal subgroup V of order 35 · 240 such that V = W ⋊S where |W| = 35 and S ∼= 4 · A5. Since the
normalizer of a Sylow 3-subgroup of 4 · A5 has a component C4 × C2, it turns out that a Sylow 3-subgroup
of HN has a non-cyclic component, a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= Fi22, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 39. From [74, Section 5.8.7 Table 5.8] and [47],
Γ has a maximal subgroup V of order 39 · 29 such that V = W ⋊(S ⋊Z) where |W| = 37, |S| = 27 and
Z ∼= S3 × S3. Therefore, W ⋊T is a subgroup of Γ where |W| = 39 and T ∼= (C2 × C2), a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= Co1, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 39. From [74, Section 5.4.2 Table 5.3], Γ has a
maximal subgroup V of order 39 · 27 such that V = W ⋊(C2 · Z) where |W| = 37, and Z ∼= S4 × S4. The
complement of a normalizer of a Sylow 3-subgroup of C2 · Z is isomorphic to C2 × C2. Therefore, there is a
subgroup W ⋊T in Γ such that |W| = 39 and T ∼= (C2 × C2), a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= Co3, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 37. From [74, Section 5.4.2 Table 5.3], Γ has a
maximal subgroup V of order 37 · 15840 such that V = W ⋊(C2 × Z) where |W| = 37, and Z ∼= M11.
The complement of a normalizer of a Sylow 3-subgroup of M11 is a semidihedral group SD8 of order 16.
Therefore, there is a subgroup W ⋊T in Γ such that |W| = 37 and T ∼= SD8, a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= Suz or Γ ∼= Ly, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 37. From [74, Section 5.6.11 Table 5.4] and
[74, Section 5.9.5] respectively, Γ has a subgroup V of order 37 · 7920 such that V = W ⋊S where |W| = 37,
and S = M11. But this leads to a contradiction as for Γ ∼= Co3.
If Γ ∼= McL, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 36. From [74, Section 5.4.2 Table 5.3], Γ has a
maximal subgroup V of order 36 · 720 such that V = W ⋊(S · C2) where |W| = 36, and S ∼= Alt6. The
complement of a normalizer of a Sylow 3-subgroup of Alt6 · C2 is isomorphic to the quaternion group Q8 of
order 16. Therefore, there is a subgroup W ⋊T in Γ such that |W| = 36 and T ∼= Q8, a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= Co2, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 37. From [74, Section 5.4.2 Table 5.3], Co2 has a
subgroup V ∼= McL. Since a Sylow 3-subgroup of V is also a Sylow 3-subgroup of Co2, the arguments used
for McL can be adapted to rule out Co2.
If Γ ∼= J2 or Γ ∼= J3, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 34 and 35, respectively. From [74, Section
5.9.2 Table 5.11], Γ has a maximal subgroup V = W ⋊C8 with |W| = 33, and |W| = 35, respectively.
Therefore, ΓP ∼= V , and hence |HP | = 8. From the second claim in Proposition 7.9, the bound (87) follows.
If Γ ∼= O′N, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 34. From [74, Section 5.9.4 Table 5.11], Γ has a
subgroup V = W ⋊S where |W| = 34 and S = Alt6, a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= M12 then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 33. From [74, Section 5.9 Table 5.11], Γ has a maximal
subgroup V of order 33 · 24 such that V = W ⋊(C2 · S) where |W| = 32, and S ∼= Sym4. The complement of
a normalizer of a Sylow 3-subgroup of C2 · Sym4 is isomorphic to the Klein group K4 of 4. Therefore, there
is a subgroup W ⋊T in Γ such that |W| = 32 and T ∼= K4, a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= M24 then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 33. From [74, Section 5.9 Table 5.11], Γ has a subgroup
isomorphic to M12. Since a Sylow 3-subgroup of M11 is also a Sylow 3-subgroup of M24, the arguments used
for M12 can be adapted to rule out M24.
If Γ ∼= J4 then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 33. From [74, Section 5.9.6], Γ has a subgroup isomorphic
to M12. Since a Sylow 3-subgroup of M11 is also a Sylow 3-subgroup of J4, the arguments used for M12 can
be adapted to rule out J4.
If Γ ∼= M11 then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 32. From [74, Section 5.3.8 Table 5.11], Γ has a
maximal subgroup V of order 32 · 24 such that V = W ⋊S where |W| = 32, and S is a semidihedral group
SD8 of order 16, a contradiction.

If Γ ∼= M22 then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 32. From [74, Section 5.3.8 Table 5.1], Γ has a maximal
subgroup isomorphic to PSL(3, 4). The complement of a normalizer of a Sylow 3-subgroup of PSL(3, 4) is
isomorphic to the Klein group K4 of 4. Therefore, there is a subgroup W ⋊T in Γ such that |W| = 32 and
T ∼= K4, a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= M23 then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 32. From [74, Section 5.3.8 Table 5.11], Γ has a
maximal subgroup isomorphic to M22. Since a Sylow 3-subgroup of M22 is also a Sylow 3-subgroup of M23,
the arguments used for M22 can be adapted to rule out M23.
If Γ ∼= HS then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 32.
From [74, Section 5.5.1 Table 5.3], Γ has a
maximal subgroup isomorphic to M22. Since a Sylow 3-subgroup of M22 is also a Sylow 3-subgroup of M23,
the arguments used for M22 can be adapted to rule out HS.

where |W| = 32, and S is a semidihedral group SD8 of order 16, a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= He then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 33. From [74, Section 5.9.3 Table 5.11], Γ has a
maximal subgroup V of order 26 · 2160 such that V = W ⋊(C3 · S) where |W| = 32, and S ∼= Sym6. The
complement of the normalizer of a Sylow 3-subgroup of C3 · Sym6 is isomorphic to the dihedral group D4 of
order 8. Therefore, there is a subgroup W ⋊T in Γ such that |W| = 32 and T ∼= D4, a contradiction.
If Γ ∼= J1, then a Sylow 3-subgroup of Γ has order 3.
From [74, Section 5.9 Table 5.11], Γ has a
maximal subgroup isomorphic to the direct product D6 × D10 of two dihedral groups of order 12 and 20,
respectively. Therefore, the complement of the normalizer of a Sylow 3-subgroup contains a Klein group K4,
a contradiction.
□

(x = ξ(t), y = η(t)) of ρ1 where ξ(t), η(t) are formal power series over K. Since ρ1 is tangent to ℓ1, we
have ξ(t) = ctα1 + . . . and η(t) = dtβ1 + . . . where c, d ∈K∗and 0 < α1 < β1 are integers. From (106),
ξ(t)mη(t)m−q +ξ(t)mη(t)m−1 −ξ(t)2m −η(t)2m is the zero formal power series. Therefore, mα1 = (m−q)β1.
Since gcd(m, q) = 1, this implies β1 ≥m and α1 ≥m−q. Now, take a branch ρ2 of C′ with center at O which
is tangent to ℓ2. Then ρ2 has a primitive branch representation (x = ξ(t), y = η(t)) with ξ(t) = ctα2 + . . .
and η(t) = dtβ2 + . . . where α2 > β2 > 0. Replacing index 1 by 2 in the above computation, the analog
argument yields that β2 ≥m and α2 ≥m + q. Let ri,j = I(ℓj ∩ρi, O) be the intersection multiplicity of
the branch ρi with the line ℓj at O. Then r1,1 = β1, r1,2 = α1, r2,1 = β2, r2,2 = α2. Since O is the unique
common point of ℓ1 with the projective closure of C′, B´ezout’s theorem [39, Theorem 3.14, 4.50] yields
2m ≥β1 + β2 ≥m + m = 2m. Therefore, ρ1 is the unique branch of C′ with center O which is tangent to
ℓ1. Similarly 2m ≥α1 + α2 ≥m −q + m + q = 2m and hence ρ2 is the unique branch of C′ with center O
which is tangent to ℓ2. Therefore, ρ1 and ρ2 are exactly the branches of C′ with center O. Thus, Y∞is the
center of exactly two branches of the projective closure of C.
Looking at the plane model C, T fixes no affine point, and hence no branch of C with center at an affine
point. Moreover, T fixes both branches ρ1 and ρ2. Since ¯
X = X/T is rational, the Deuring-Shafarevic
formula applied to T reads
γ(X) −1 = q(0 −1) + 2(q −1)
whence γ(X) = q −1.
For every m-th root of unity λ, u := (x, y) 7→(λx, y) is in Aut(X). They form a cyclic group U of order
m. Moreover, v := (x, y) 7→(1/x, y) is in Aut(X) and U together with v generate a dihedral group V of
order 2m. Since ⟨T, V ⟩= T × V , we end up with a subgroup of Aut(X) of order 2mq which is the direct
product of an elementary abelian p-group of order q with a dihedral group of order 2m.
